=== runbook
# QA client credential — out-of-band seeding runbook

**Format change (handoff-03-auth.md v7):** `gen-argon2-hash.go` now emits a PHC-format hash instead of the earlier `<base64-salt>$<base64-hash>` encoding — the two are not interchangeable. Any row see

Per Priya's ruling on LT-51: "migrations carry reference data, never credentials" — the QA client row in `oauth_client` is seeded by hand, directly against the issuer database's own migrator role, nev

## 1. Generate the secret and its Argon2id hash

```
CLIENT_SECRET=$(openssl rand -hex 24)
go run deploy/gen-argon2-hash.go "$CLIENT_SECRET" > /tmp/qa-hash.txt
HASH=$(cat /tmp/qa-hash.txt)
rm -f /tmp/qa-hash.txt
```

`deploy/gen-argon2-hash.go` produces a standard PHC-format Argon2id hash (`«REDACTED-ARGON2»`) — the same self-describing encoding `user_credential.secret` already uses, a

**Give `$CLIENT_SECRET` to whoever will run the PO review script and nowhere else** — it is the Basic-auth password for `POST /auth/token`. It cannot be recovered from `$HASH` afterward; if lost, gene

## 2. Insert the row

```
MIGRATOR_DSN=$(kubectl -n loginid-takehome get secret \
  issuer-db-migrator-credential -o jsonpath='{.data.dsn}' | base64 -d)

psql "$MIGRATOR_DSN" -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -c "
  INSERT INTO oauth_client (client_id, name, client_secret_hash, secret_state, granted_scopes, audience, status)
  VALUES ('qa-review-client', 'PO review script', '$HASH', 'set', ARRAY['profile:read:own'], 'loginid-api-service', 'active');
"

unset MIGRATOR_DSN CLIENT_SECRET HASH
```

`granted_scopes` is a single-element array (`profile:read:own`) — the schema's own `ck_oauth_client_granted_scopes_single` CHECK requires exactly one, matching this project's one-credential-per-scope 

**`audience` must be the literal `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` value api-service actually verifies against — `loginid-api-service`, per `PLANNING.md`'s config surface — not the Kubernetes Service name `api-serv

## 3. Confirm it without re-exposing the secret

```
kubectl -n loginid-takehome exec postgres-0 -- env PGPASSWORD="$(kubectl -n loginid-takehome get secret issuer-db-runtime-credential -o jsonpath='{.data.password}' | base64 -d)" \
  psql "sslmode=require host=localhost user=issuer_runtime dbname=issuer" \
  -c "SELECT client_id, secret_state, status FROM oauth_client WHERE client_id = 'qa-review-client';"
```

Expect one row, `secret_state = 'set'`, `status = 'active'` — confirms the insert landed and that the runtime role (the one `api-service-issuer` actually connects as at request time) can read it, with

## Rotation / revocation

- **Rotate:** repeat step 1 for a new secret, then `UPDATE oauth_client SET client_secret_hash = '$HASH' WHERE client_id = 'qa-review-client';` via the migrator DSN.
- **Revoke:** `UPDATE oauth_client SET secret_state = 'revoked', client_secret_hash = NULL WHERE client_id = 'qa-review-client';` — the schema's tying CHECK requires `client_secret_hash IS NULL` whene

# Planning Approach: Claude-Architecture Recommendations for This Team

Author: Marcus Ilori (02-ai-security-architecture), synthesizing input gathered directly from all four other track owners — naomi-voss (01), renata-cole (03), theo-bergman (04), priya-nandakumar (05) — plus this track's own perspective and `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md`. Where the tracks disagreed or emphasized different things, I say so rather than averaging them into a mush; where they independently converged (which happened more than I expected), I note that too, because five agents with separate contexts landing on the same answer is itself weak evidence the answer is right.

Throughout, I distinguish Anthropic-documented guidance from my own judgment applied to this specific project. Where I say "Anthropic recommends," that traces to a specific source in `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md`. Where I say "my view" or "I'd recommend," that's this project's reasoning, not a citation.

## 1. Models and effort levels, by role

The knobs: model ∈ {fable, opus, sonnet, haiku}, effort ∈ {low, medium, high, xhigh, max}. Warren has already set the orchestrator (team-lead) to fable at high. My reasoning below takes that as fixed and reasons about everyone else relative to it.

## 5. Orchestration pattern catalog

Each pattern is a small protocol: roles, sequence, the artifact it produces, when it earns its cost, and where it is waste. Cost is in agent-turns (one hire invocation and reply). Every run ends in a durable file in the track directory (`<track>/decisions/<topic>.md`: positions, rebuttals, the lead's synthesis and decision) — chat is not the record. The AI-architecture owner (02) ratifies and may amend this catalog. *Ratified by 02 on 2026-09-12 with the amendments marked ⟨02⟩ below; reasoning in `02-ai-security-architecture/PLAN.md` §0.*

⟨02⟩ **All patterns:** every decision artifact ends with a footer recording the model each seat ran on and the number of hire turns consumed, so `ai-workflow-narrative.md` can cite measured cost rather than intent.

**Adversarial pair.** Proposer drafts; Designated Skeptic writes numbered objections; Proposer revises; lead rules. 3 turns + lead. ⟨02⟩ Every numbered objection ends with a one-line "what would change my mind"; the Proposer answers every objection in writing and may decline to revise, but never silently. Artifact: the decision with an "objections considered" section. *Use:* search-API authorization scoping (`profile:search` vs `profile:read:own`, object-level policy); the `Search()` query shape in `05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md`.

**Red team / blue team.** Adversary writes an attack tree against one surface; Principled Architect answers each leaf with a control or an explicitly accepted risk; lead scores residual risk. 2–3 turns. ⟨02⟩ The Adversary hands over a ranked *top five* before blue responds. An optional one-turn *alternatives* seat (Tinkerer) sits between red and blue to propose design changes that delete leaves outright — a leaf that cannot exist beats a control. Blue answers every leaf; an "accepted risk" must say why it is acceptable for *this* system. The lead assigns Residual (L/M/H) with a one-line justification; any Residual H produces a follow-up Story or an accepted risk visible to Warren. Table shape: `02-ai-security-architecture/planning-approach.md` Appendix A. Artifact: attack-tree table appended to `02-ai-security-architecture/connector-security.md`. *Use:* the connector token lifecycle only (TTL, at-rest encryption, per-vendor keying, fail-closed behavior). *Waste:* DAO CRUD, Dockerfiles.

**Three hats.** Tinkerer (optimist), Veteran (pessimist), Spreadsheet or Minimalist (pragmatist) each write ≤300 words on one choice, in parallel; lead synthesizes. 3 turns + lead. *Use:* the multi-DB abstraction (per-driver implementations vs. dialect branches vs. codegen); Go layout (one binary vs. two). A lead may take a hat.

**Rotating devil's advocate.** A standing "Objection" seat that moves to a different hire per decision so nobody becomes "the negative one". 1 turn per decision, appended as a short Objection block. *Use:* 03's implementation calls (error semantics, context propagation, test doubles). Cheapest pattern; run it often.

**Structured written debate.** Position → rebuttal → lead synthesis, with both positions preserved in the record. 3 turns. ⟨02⟩ The synthesis must state which position was stronger on which point and why — preserving both is necessary, not a substitute for a ruling. *Use:* 01's central framing (is a password baseline a deliberate simplification or a contradiction at a passkey company?); 02's core claim in `ai-workflow-narrative.md`.

**Newcomer's question.** Newcomer reads a finished hand-off cold and lists what it could not act on without re-deriving the sender's reasoning; the author fixes. 1–2 turns. *Use:* every 05→03 and 02→03 hand-off, the README, `PLANNING.md`. This directly tests the project's hand-off standard and is the highest-ROI pattern here.

**Where patterns are waste.** Never debate what the assignment text fixes (endpoint paths, JSON bodies). 04's track is small: one Newcomer's-question pass on 03's config surface and 02's secrets inventory, nothing more.

## 6. Hire-creation procedure (after team-lead's explicit "hiring go")

1. Confirm the archetype is still available under the rules above.
2. Create the persona **text-first** with profile-gen: `write_profile.py --fields-file <fields.json> --root <track-dir> --output file --assets tracked` — `personality` written to §4; `image` pre-declared as `profiles/<slug>/<slug>.png`; `display.autostart: false`; `generation.backend: comfyui` with the intended portrait prompt and negative prompt. The persona lands at `<track-dir>/profiles/<slug>/<slug>.md`. **Never add a hire as a `profile-gen` marker in any `CLAUDE.md`** — the autostart hook displays every marker it finds.
3. Create `.claude/agents/<slug>.md` from `.claude/AGENT_TEMPLATE.md` (kept outside `.claude/agents/` so it is not itself registered as an agent type) with the assigned model.
4. Append the registry row below with `Portrait: queued`.
5. **Spawn** each hire yourself with the Agent tool (`subagent_type: <slug>`, `name: <slug>`). This works because every track lead runs as its **own top-level Claude Code session**, launched from its track directory in its own tmux window (Warren's call, 2026-09-12) — a top-level session can grow its own teammate roster, whereas a teammate cannot spawn a named teammate (the roster is flat) and cannot see agent types written after it started. Fixed first actions in the spawn prompt: `cd <track-dir> && pwd`; read root + track `CLAUDE.md`, `./PLAN.md`, own persona; run the `show_profile.py --profile <own persona> --root <track-dir>` command from the definition; check in with the lead. Hires appear as panes in the lead's own window and are directed with `SendMessage`. Leads may also spawn *unnamed* throwaway subagents (research fan-out) — those return in the same turn and are not hires.

Team-lead (the PM session) runs `scripts/portrait-queue.sh` in the background; it renders queued portraits one at a time on the shared ComfyUI box and flips each row to `done`. Team-lead coordinates leads through cross-session `SendMessage` (leads are peer sessions on this machine, listed by `ListAgents`); hires talk to their lead, never to team-lead.

7:## 1. Models and effort levels, by role
11:**Orchestrator / PM (team-lead) — fable, high.** Confirmed correct, not just accepted. The orchestrator holds the only cross-track state that matters (scope boundaries, status, "who's blocked on whom") and makes judgment calls that propagate into every track if wrong — a boundary misjudged here (e.g., letting infra silently absorb a decision that was security's to make) costs five agents' worth of rework, not one. That's the single seat in this project where the highest model ceiling earns its cost. I would not go to xhigh/max for this role: xhigh/max effort spends more test-time compute on deeper deliberation per response, and orchestration work here is mostly reading condensed hand-offs and delegating — breadth of correct judgment across many small decisions, not depth on one hard problem. High is the right ceiling for that shape of work.
13:**Track owners (the five of us, in planning/design mode) — sonnet, medium-to-high, track-dependent.** This is my own judgment, and it's not uniform across tracks — the five tracks are not equally deep:
19:**Throwaway research subagents (fact-gathering, doc lookups, exploring existing code) — haiku, low, or sonnet/low if some judgment is needed.** Naomi's own workflow already does this (haiku/sonnet-low fanned out for industry facts, then one sonnet/medium synthesis pass) and it generalizes: per Anthropic's orchestrator-worker pattern, a subagent's exploration noise never re-enters the coordinator's context — only its condensed output does — so there's no reason to pay for a stronger model on a narrow, verifiable, throwaway task. Fan these out in parallel; that's where cheap-and-fast is unambiguously the right call.
21:**Implementation (writing the actual Go code — DAO, REST handlers, connector) — sonnet, medium-to-high; opus only for one narrow piece.** Renata, who owns this work, put it as sonnet/medium-high: once upstream contracts land, this is CRUD/REST/HTTP-client code against clear specs — not novel enough for opus, but not so trivial that low-effort stubs are acceptable when "reads as intentional engineering" is the grading criterion. Tests (table-driven Go, mocked DB/HTTP) are sonnet/medium. I agree with all of that, and it changes my earlier position: I had planned to reserve opus/high for implementing the DAO's multi-database interface, but if Priya delivers the interface as actual Go signatures (see hand-off structure, §2), the hard decision has already been made upstream at opus/high and implementing against it is sonnet work. That leaves the connector's token lifecycle logic (acquire, cache, refresh, never-persist-in-plaintext) as the one implementation surface where I'd still spend opus/high — the correctness bar is a security property, not just a passing test, and the assignment's question 3 is where a subtle bug most directly contradicts this track's own threat model. Anthropic's own internal eval (research doc §3) found an Opus-lead/Sonnet-subagent configuration beat a single-agent Opus baseline by 90.2% at materially higher token cost; the lesson I take is "spend the expensive model where a wrong foundational call is costly to reverse," not "use Opus everywhere."
23:**Review/verification passes — high effort always; independence scaled to stakes.** Two positions came in here, and they're compatible rather than contradictory. Renata wants a high-effort review pass on her own output before calling it done, same model — catching a busted interface boundary late costs more than the tokens. That's right for engineering surfaces. My addition, Anthropic-motivated but my own extension: their multi-agent paper's rationale for parallel independent subagents is that separation of concerns "reduces path dependency" — an agent reviewing its own output inherits its own blind spots. Resolving the two explicitly: Renata's same-session high-effort self-review is the *first* pass on everything she produces — it's cheap, it catches the interface-boundary class of bug she's worried about, and there's no reason to skip it. For the security-graded surfaces specifically (credential storage design, token handling, the auth/authz scheme, and the connector code that implements them), a *second* pass in a genuinely independent session is added on top, because those are exactly what LoginID said they're grading the reasoning quality of, and a self-review cannot by construction catch an assumption the author didn't know they were making. For everything else (infra sketches, product prose, routine handlers), the self-review alone is proportionate. This is a take-home POC, not a production deploy, and calibrating review independence to actual stakes is part of the "why," not corner-cutting.
25:**Fable reserved narrowly.** I would not spread fable across all five track-owner seats — that's five top-model seats purchased for a take-home whose own cited evidence (the Opus-lead/Sonnet-worker eval) argues for concentrating the strongest model at the lead and running capable-but-cheaper models at the worker tier. The one place I'd consider a second fable seat: a final cross-track consistency pass, run once near submission, checking the five tracks' finished deliverables against each other for contradictions (the exact class of problem Naomi flagged — two tracks independently deciding what `/identity` returns). That's a single, short-lived, high-value use of the top model, not a standing role.
27:## 2. Architecture pattern for planning-phase coordination
29:**Default: orchestrator-worker, not peer-to-peer mesh.** This is Anthropic's own documented pattern (research doc §3 — lead agent decomposes, spawns scoped subagents, subagents report back condensed findings, lead synthesizes) and Naomi independently converged on the same recommendation from the product side: five agents cross-talking as a full mesh during planning is more collision risk than value. Status and scope decisions should route through team-lead.
31:**Exception: direct track-to-track handoffs on a specific shared decision.** A pure hub-and-spoke model is wrong for one case — two tracks with a direct, narrow dependency on each other's specific decision. This track and data-ops already did this, and Priya asked that it be recorded here as the worked example: we coordinated directly on modeling `user_credential.method` as a lookup table rather than a native enum, because that's a two-party decision (her schema, my method-aware credential-storage design), not a project-wide one, and routing it through team-lead both directions would have added latency for no benefit. Renata, by contrast, explicitly doesn't want peer chatter with 01 or 04 — she wants clean hand-offs from 02 and 05 that she reads once and implements against. Both are right; the difference is whether a genuine two-party decision exists. The rule I'd formalize: point-to-point messaging is fine for a specific, bounded, two-track decision; it must still land in a durable file (see §3) as the source of truth, not live only in chat history; and it doesn't substitute for the orchestrator's overall view of status.
33:**Plan mode.** Narrower than I first drafted, after Renata's input. She doesn't want plan mode for her own track — the plan already exists in the assignment text, and if upstream tracks hand her concrete contracts there's nothing left to plan that a plan-mode pass would catch. I think that's correct *conditional on the hand-offs being concrete enough* (see next paragraph); if 05 hands her a prose strategy instead of interface signatures, plan mode before the DAO becomes necessary again because she'd be making design decisions that aren't hers. So the rule: plan mode gates decisions that constrain another track — API contract shapes, schema fields, token formats, the Go interface itself — at the track that owns the decision, before it's declared stable. It does not gate implementation against an already-concrete contract. For a track whose CLAUDE.md brief is already specific and self-contained (per the root file's own stated goal), formal plan-mode approval on top of that brief is redundant — the brief already is the approved plan.
35:**Fork vs. fresh scoped agent.** Fork when a task needs the current session's accumulated context and would lose something real by starting clean (e.g., resuming a security review with everything already threat-modeled in-session). Fresh scoped agent when the task is genuinely independent and carrying forward unrelated context would only dilute it (a documentation lookup, a track that hasn't started). This project's five persistent track-owner teammates are, at a coarser grain, already an instance of "fresh scoped agent": each seeded only with the root `CLAUDE.md` plus its own track file, not all five tracks' accumulated history — that's Anthropic's just-in-time retrieval principle (research doc §2, technique 4) applied at the team-structure level, not just the subagent level.
37:**Hand-off structure.** Per the research doc (§2/§3), Anthropic's own subagents target roughly 1,000–2,000 tokens of condensed findings passed to a coordinator — never a raw transcript. Concretely here: hand-offs happen through each track's `CLAUDE.md` Status section and named deliverable files, referenced by relative path, exactly as the root file already mandates ("cross-reference other tracks by relative path... rather than restating their content"). A `SendMessage` between tracks signals readiness or asks a targeted question — it is not the transport for the actual content, and Renata was explicit that she wants upstream decisions written somewhere durable, not relayed verbally through team-lead.
43:**Context and cache management over a multi-hour project.** Two concrete disciplines, both sourced from the research doc's context-engineering section: (1) keep each track's `CLAUDE.md` stable within a working session — edit it at the start or end of a turn, not interleaved mid-task, because an edit to a file currently loaded in context invalidates that cached prefix (research doc §4/§5); (2) when a track's own work legitimately grows long across multiple similar passes (data-ops iterating across DB backends is the clearest case in this project), compact deliberately — write a short settled-decisions summary to a file and start the next pass fresh from that summary rather than carrying the full multi-turn history forward (§2, technique 1). Neither of these is exotic; they're the direct, mechanical application of Anthropic's documented "context rot" mitigation to this project's actual shape of work.
45:**Planning dependency order across tracks.** All five tracks described their dependencies independently and the graph they describe is consistent — nobody claimed a dependency someone else denied. Assembled:
51:## 3. Where the shared planning artifact lives
55:**Recommendation:** a root-level `PLANNING.md`, purely as a thin cross-track coordination board — one table row per track: status, blocking-on, blocked-by, pointer to that track's planning notes, last-updated — plus, per Theo's specific ask, a single "service boundaries" row or short section that 03 owns and 04 reads, so infra isn't polling engineering's `CLAUDE.md` for updates. Short, cheap to write, cheap to skim, genuinely low collision risk because each row has exactly one writer. The actual reasoning — the "why," which is most of what LoginID is grading — stays where it already lives, in each track's own files, referenced by relative path. This keeps the artifact consistent with the root `CLAUDE.md`'s existing rule (cross-reference, don't restate) instead of introducing a second place the same content has to be kept in sync.

**Planning dependency order across tracks.** All five tracks described their dependencies independently and the graph they describe is consistent — nobody claimed a dependency someone else denied. Assembled:

1. **First wave, in parallel: product/research (01), security (02), data-ops (05).** None of the three blocks on another. Naomi's framing depends only on the assignment text; my threat model and auth design needed the assignment text plus the one bounded coordination point with 05 (already resolved); Priya states 05 does not block on 01 or 02. Two nuances worth naming. First, Naomi regards 01 as upstream of everyone, while Priya and Renata both say 01 can run "anytime, independently" — those reconcile: nothing *hard*-blocks on 01, but her `api-connector-design-rationale.md` should be designated the single source of truth for field/endpoint shape so engineering isn't re-deriving it, which is a soft dependency (read it before finalizing, not before starting). The assignment text already specifies the question-2/3 endpoint shapes fairly concretely, so the drift risk is real but narrower than a green-field API would carry. Second, 05 is the critical-path member of this wave — it hard-blocks 03 and partially blocks 04 — so if anything in the first wave gets priority attention, it's the schema and interface contract.
2. **Engineering (03) has a split start.** Renata drew a useful distinction: 05 is a hard blocker for the DAO code, but 02 is not a blocker for her early scaffolding — project structure, Go module layout, route wiring, stub handlers can begin while the auth decision is still settling, and she needs 02 only before finalizing the middleware. So 03 starts scaffolding as soon as the first wave is underway and starts the DAO the moment 05's contract is stable — "stable" meaning unlikely to be rewritten, not fully polished. Waiting for perfection would idle the track that owns the largest deliverable.
3. **Infra (04) is last**, exactly as Theo described his own role: reviewer/consumer during planning (read the other four tracks' files, flag anything infra-shaped so it isn't silently owned elsewhere), producer only once three specific things exist — 03's service topology, config surface, and DAO driver-selection mechanism; 02's secrets inventory and never-log list; 05's migration tool/approach. Building infra design against boundaries that don't exist yet is fiction dressed as a deliverable, in Theo's words, and I don't have a better way to put it.

## 3. Where the shared planning artifact lives

Three tracks offered a view and they converge. Naomi proposed a single root-level `PLANNING.md` with one section per track, each track editing only its own. Theo wants one owner per section, no shared free-text blob five agents edit concurrently, and suggested a single index file linking to each track's own planning notes — matching the existing directory structure. Renata wants upstream decisions in the owning track's `CLAUDE.md` or a shared design doc she reads once. The common thread is one-owner-per-unit and a thin index, and I'd adopt Theo's shape over Naomi's for two reasons: first, even with an "own section" discipline, five agents live-editing one shared file in a short window is still a collision surface that a table of independent rows avoids more cleanly than prose sections do; second, and more importantly, this project already has a durable decision-record layer — each track's own `CLAUDE.md` plus its deliverable files — and duplicating design content into a second file risks the two drifting apart, which is a worse failure mode than the collision risk it's meant to solve.

**Recommendation:** a root-level `PLANNING.md`, purely as a thin cross-track coordination board — one table row per track: status, blocking-on, blocked-by, pointer to that track's planning notes, last-updated — plus, per Theo's specific ask, a single "service boundaries" row or short section that 03 owns and 04 reads, so infra isn't polling engineering's `CLAUDE.md` for updates. Short, cheap to write, cheap to skim, genuinely low collision risk because each row has exactly one writer. The actual reasoning — the "why," which is most of what LoginID is grading — stays where it already lives, in each track's own files, referenced by relative path. This keeps the artifact consistent with the root `CLAUDE.md`'s existing rule (cross-reference, don't restate) instead of introducing a second place the same content has to be kept in sync.

## Sources

All Anthropic-attributed claims above trace to `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md` in this directory, specifically: the orchestrator-worker pattern and the Opus-lead/Sonnet-subagent eval (§3, "How we built our multi-agent research system"), the context-rot/compaction/structured-note-taking/just-in-time-retrieval framework (§2, "Effective context engineering for AI agents"), and the prompt-caching mechanics around prefix stability (§4, Anthropic's prompt-caching docs). The model/effort-to-role pairings, the hub-and-spoke-with-exceptions coordination rule, the plan-mode usage rule, and the planning-artifact shape are this track's own judgment applied to this project — not claims of separate Anthropic endorsement.

## Appendix A — Red team / blue team attack-tree template

Referenced from `../CASTING.md` §5. One table per surface; the completed table is appended to that surface's design document (for the connector token lifecycle, `connector-security.md`), and the full positions, rebuttals, and ruling live in `decisions/<topic>.md`.

| # | Leaf (attack path toward the goal) | Precondition (what the attacker must already hold) | Likelihood L/M/H | Impact L/M/H | Blue response: Control *or* Accepted risk | Owner (02 requirement / 03 implements / 04 mechanism) | Residual L/M/H | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Rules of use: the Adversary ranks the top five leaves before blue responds, and the ranking is recorded. The Tinkerer's optional alternatives turn may delete a leaf by design change; a deleted leaf is kept in the table with "eliminated by design: …" in the Blue column so the reasoning survives. Blue answers every remaining leaf; "accepted risk" must say why it is acceptable for this system, not in general. The lead assigns Residual with a one-line justification in the record. Any Residual **H** produces either a follow-up Story or an accepted risk stated plainly enough that Warren can see it in the artifact. The Source column names the primary source behind the control (RFC, OWASP, NIST) or reads "judgment".

## Appendix B — Harness constraints that modify §1's tiers

Recorded after `PLAN.md` "Verified mechanics" was checked against `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md` (no contradictions found). These are harness facts, verified by team-lead against the Claude Code docs, that change how §1 is implemented:

1. There is no per-agent effort key in agent definitions; teammates inherit the lead session's effort (currently `high`), and the only per-model lever is `modelSettings.<model>.effortLevel` in `.claude/settings.json`, set to `low` for haiku only. Consequence: the sonnet-medium tier in §1 collapses to sonnet-high — accepted, since it errs upward on the tracks that matter — and the "sonnet/low" research option is unavailable. Research fan-out is haiku/low, or sonnet at high when judgment is needed, which costs more than §1 planned and should be used sparingly.
2. Hires are one level below leads and do not spawn subagents (spawn-depth cap); all research fan-out is run by leads. This is consistent with §2's orchestrator-worker rule, just enforced by the harness rather than by convention.
3. §4-style prompt-cache discipline applies one level down: a hire's `.claude/agents/<slug>.md` is part of that hire's stable prefix, so it is edited only at spawn boundaries, never while the hire is running.
4. The per-agent `PreToolUse` hook mechanism and agent-frontmatter facts are outside this track's research scope; they are recorded here as verified by team-lead, not independently by 02.
=================== NARRATIVE
# AI Tooling & Workflow Narrative

Owner: Marcus Ilori (`02-ai-security-architecture`). This is the full answer to LoginID's explicit ask ("describe any tool, framework, or AI used") — other tracks note their own AI usage briefly and point here for depth, per `../CLAUDE.md`.

This document states what was done, distinguishes what's independently documented by Anthropic from what this project composed on its own, and is deliberately precise about that boundary rather than overclaiming an official pattern that doesn't exist. Full source-by-source detail lives in `research-claude-architecture-best-practices.md`; this document is the applied summary a reviewer can read without the research doc.

## Tool

**Claude Code**, Anthropic's agentic CLI. Chosen because the deliverable itself is an exercise in demonstrating AI-architect judgment — using the tool that is itself the subject of the "how did you use AI" question, rather than a general-purpose chat interface, lets the workflow be inspected as part of the submission.

## Structure: a root brief plus five track-scoped briefs

The repository is organized as one root `CLAUDE.md` (the assignment text, track boundaries, cross-track working principles) plus five track directories, each with its own `CLAUDE.md` scoped to a distinct slice of the problem (product/industry framing, this track's security architecture, engineering delivery, infra/devops, data ops). Each track file is written to be self-contained enough that a fresh agent with no other conversation history can pick it up correctly given only that file plus the root file.

**What this composes, precisely:**

1. **The `CLAUDE.md` mechanism itself** is Anthropic's own, documented at https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory — auto-loaded at session start, meant to be treated as living documentation, kept short and specific.
2. **The decision to fan that mechanism out per-directory, with a stable root file for cross-cutting state**, is this project's own application of a general principle Anthropic documents separately in "Effective context engineering for AI agents" (https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents): structured notes persisted to files outside the live context window, retrieved just-in-time rather than all loaded upfront, so a track's subagent carries only the ~1-2 files it actually needs rather than the whole project's accumulated context.

I'm stating this distinction explicitly because it matters to how this narrative should be read: Anthropic has not published a named "root CLAUDE.md + per-directory CLAUDE.md" pattern, and claiming they had would be exactly the kind of unsupported assertion this track's whole discipline argues against. What's true and defensible is narrower and still worth the credit — this is a reasonable, motivated composition of two things Anthropic documented separately.

## Orchestration: scoped subagents per track, not one long single-context session

Each track is intended to be worked by an agent (in practice here, a persona-bearing teammate agent per track — see each track's `profiles/` entry) that reads the root file plus its own track file and works within that scope, rather than one agent holding all five tracks' context simultaneously and switching between them. This follows the orchestrator-worker pattern Anthropic describes in "How we built our multi-agent research system" (https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system): a coordinating layer decomposes work into distinct-objective, distinct-tool-scope units; each unit works its own trajectory; only condensed results cross back, not raw exploration. Anthropic's own internal figure for a subagent-to-coordinator handoff is roughly 1,000-2,000 tokens of distilled findings — the discipline this project applies at track boundaries is the same: a track's `CLAUDE.md` plus its finished deliverable files (this document included) are what another track reads, never a transcript of how those conclusions were reached.

Concretely for this track: the threat model, the API auth/authz design, and the connector security design (`threat-model.md`, `api-auth-design.md`, `connector-security.md`) are the condensed artifacts `03-engineering-delivery` needs to implement against. It should read those files, not need this track's reasoning replayed turn-by-turn.

## The org layer: personas, patterns, and debates (added after Phase 2 execution)

The structure above (root brief, five track briefs, scoped subagents) is the skeleton. What actually ran inside it, once Warren authorized hiring, was a second layer this project built on top of Anthropic's documented patterns rather than found in them: each of the five track leads hired a small sub-team of persona-bearing agents — this track hired four (Tomasz Wrede, an Adversary; Helena Marsh, a Principled Architect; Felix Adebayo, a Tinkerer and this lead's deliberate temperament opposite; Ingrid Solano, a Designated Skeptic) — and ran a small catalog of named orchestration patterns (red team/blue team, adversarial pair, structured written debate, independent second review, rotating devil's advocate, three hats) against specific design decisions, each ending in a durable decision file under `decisions/`. **This entire layer — the personas, the variance rules in `../CASTING.md`, the pattern catalog, the rule that every decision artifact records its own model/turn-count footer — is this project's own design, not an Anthropic-documented technique.** It's a deliberate, load-bearing extension built on top of the orchestrator-worker pattern (§"Orchestration" above), motivated by a specific risk Anthropic's own writing doesn't have to worry about but a five-lead org does: five track leads (and the PM) starting from the same base model land in the same temperament quadrant by default, and agreement between agents who all think alike is not evidence of a good decision.

### Attribution by persona, into the implementation phase (added 2026-09-12, Warren's rule)

The model/turn-count footer discipline described above (every decision artifact names which seat ran on which model) extends into implementation: every commit, PR, and Jira ticket touching this track's work states who did it, by persona name, not by "the lead" standing in for a hire. An independent second review posted on a PR is signed `Independent review: <hire name> (02, <archetype>) — <findings>` — Ingrid Solano, Helena Marsh, or Tomasz Wrede specifically, whichever seat actually ran the review, the same way S8's decision records already name the individual reviewer rather than "the track." The same signed text is the Jira comment, not a paraphrase of it. This is the same principle the pattern catalog already runs on — a reader should be able to trace which seat produced which finding — carried one layer further, from design documents into the commits and tickets those documents produce.

This is not a claim that multi-agent debate produces better designs than a single strong session **in general** — that claim was itself put to a structured written debate in this track (S5, `decisions/ai-workflow-claim-debate.md`) and the ruling is reproduced faithfully below, including the position that lost ground. What can be stated and evidenced:

- **S3 (red/blue, connector token lifecycle):** Tomasz's red seat produced five ranked attack leaves, each shaped as "add a control" (encrypt the cache harder, bind the token, rate-limit more). Felix's alternatives seat — a role that exists specifically to ask "does the thing being protected need to exist" rather than "is the control sufficient" — proposed deleting two of those leaves outright by removing the token cache entirely (fetch-per-call instead of cache-with-a-TTL), rather than hardening it. That's a concrete design change this document can point to that would not have existed without a seat whose brief was structurally different from the seat before it. Tomasz's own rebuttal turn on the remaining three leaves then surfaced a *new* attack variant on leaf 1 (connection reuse attaching one request's token to another, unrelated to the cache Felix had just deleted) that Helena's blue answer hadn't addressed, closed with a new control; conceded leaf 2 cleanly with no new finding; and tightened leaf 3's accepted-risk boundary. **Corrected tally (cross-track consistency pass, F39 — the earlier count of "2 hits, 1 tightening, 2 null" undercounted the rebuttal turn's own catch): 2 leaves deleted by design change, 1 new variant surfaced and closed by the rebuttal turn, 1 boundary tightened, 1 null (leaf 2's clean concession).** The correction matters for exactly the reason this section exists — a narrative reporting its own evidence has to get the evidence right, including when getting it right makes the pattern's case stronger, not weaker.
- **S8 (independent second review, three security-graded documents) — the most valuable process evidence in this track, worth stating as a general lesson rather than just an incident report.** `PLAN.md`'s original static roster assigned Helena to review both `threat-model.md` and `api-auth-design.md`. By the time S8 actually ran, that assignment was stale: Helena had since become S4's Proposer and authored the very scope-vocabulary and policy content in `api-auth-design.md` that S8 was meant to check, and had also been S3's blue seat authoring the controls in `connector-security.md`. Reviewing either would have been an author re-reviewing their own work under the label "independent review." **The lesson: independence is a property that has to be checked at the moment a review actually runs, not a role assigned once at planning time and trusted to still hold** — a five-track org where the same small hire roster accumulates authorship across successive debates will decay its own independence guarantees exactly this way if nobody re-checks before each review. Caught here before running (Helena reassigned to `threat-model.md` only, a document she never touched; Tomasz — clean, no Phase 2 involvement in this document — took `api-auth-design.md`; Ingrid kept `connector-security.md`, unaffected), not after.

  With independence actually verified, the reviews earned their cost. **The concrete example worth leading with:** Tomasz found that the decomposition-resistant rate-limit counter S4 had just finished designing — specifically as Ingrid's own fix to her objection 4 in that same debate — was a silent no-op for `profile:read:any`, the scope with the broadest blast radius, because its exemption clause ("without a fresh `reason_code`") was unconditionally satisfied by a scope that already requires a `reason_code` on every call by design. Neither Helena (who wrote the fix) nor Ingrid (whose objection it answered) caught this, because both were reasoning inside "does this satisfy the objection as stated" — the same self-referential blind spot independent review exists to catch, now demonstrated on the review pattern's own prior output, one debate later. Ingrid's separate finding on `connector-security.md` — that a document entirely about protecting a vendor token's custody said nothing about who may call the connector holding it, or what the token authorizes once used — is a different class of question from the one the S3 red/blue run spent its effort on, for the same underlying reason: every S3 participant was already inside the "protect the token" frame. **Corrected tally (cross-track consistency pass, F40 — the earlier "4+4+2=10" undercounted `threat-model.md`'s own list of five items): 11 items across 3 documents — 10 defects plus 1 completeness note — 0 declined, 0 null reviews.** Both numbers belong in this document, not just the ones favorable to the claim.
- **Theo's Newcomer cold-read of `handoff-04-secrets.md` (04's track, not this one, but this track's document):** two

# Casting Registry — hiring rules, archetypes, and orchestration patterns

Every agent in this project is a "hire" with a profile-gen persona: a name, a temperament, a plausible professional history, a technical background, and a stated blind spot. This file is how the org keeps those hires **varied across teams, not just within one** — five leads who all hire clones of themselves would produce agreement, and agreement is not what a design review is for. Team-lead (Dana Whitfield) owns everything above the registry table; each lead owns its own rows.

Why this exists, in one sentence: the five track leads and the PM all sit in the same temperament quadrant (serious, careful, rigorous), so variance has to be engineered in deliberately or it will not happen.

## 1. Archetype palette

Thirteen archetypes. Each has a characteristic blind spot on purpose — the lead pairs against it. Playful and quirky is a legitimate professional register here; caricature is not.

| Archetype | Temperament sketch | Blind spot | Pairs well with |
|---|---|---|---|
| Principled Architect | standards-first, cites RFC section numbers, reasons threat-model-down | over-specifies; mistakes thoroughness for done | security controls, contracts |
| Tinkerer (silly & innovative) | playful, analogy-driven, sketches three odd alternatives before defending one | novelty bias; underweights boring proven controls | any decision that needs alternatives |
| Designated Skeptic | argues against the room by default, politely | objections without a counter-proposal | authz scoping, abstraction choices |
| Consensus Weaver | finds the shared premise, writes the synthesis | papers over real disagreement | decision records, hand-off prose |
| Spreadsheet | wants a number or a table; distrusts adjectives | measures only what is measurable | sizing, TTLs, retention windows |
| Storyteller | thinks in user journeys and narrative arcs | charming beats correct | product framing, README, use cases |
| Veteran | "I watched this fail in prod in 2019" | fights the last war; pattern-matches too fast | token lifecycle, migrations, ops |
| Newcomer | bright, asks why, assumes no jargon | doesn't know what's already settled | legibility tests on every hand-off |
| Detail Hawk | nullable-vs-required, off-by-one, error semantics | cannot see the forest | schema, DDL, code review |
| Systems Cartographer | draws boundaries and flows; sees interactions | abstracts past the concrete | multi-DB abstraction, service topology |
| Adversary | thinks like an attacker, enjoys it | sees threats everywhere, no prioritization | connector, search API |
| Minimalist | "what if we didn't build this?" | deletes something load-bearing | infra, scope control |
| Enthusiast | bold, optimistic, wants the promising thing tried | discounts operational cost | counterweight to deadpan/cautious leads |

Temperament tags used in the registry: `serious` / `playful`, `cautious` / `bold`.

## 2. Variance rules (enforced by team-lead at the hiring gate, reading all rosters together)

1. No archetype more than **twice org-wide**, and never twice within one team.
2. No two leads hire the same archetype for the same **function** — two "Detail Hawk reviewers" is duplication, not variance. An archetype used twice must do different work (e.g., Detail Hawk as code reviewer in 03 and as DDL/nullability reviewer in 05).
3. Every team includes its lead's **temperament opposite**: Naomi (curious narrative analyst) → Spreadsheet; Marcus (rigorous citer) → Tinkerer; Renata (clarity-over-cleverness shipper) → Enthusiast; Theo (deadpan minimalist) → Enthusiast or Newcomer; Priya (schema-first PII guardian) → Storyteller.
4. Org-wide, at least **three** hires are tagged `playful` + `bold`, because zero leads are.
5. Each roster spans at least two temperament axes (serious↔playful and cautious↔bold), not one.
6. Rosters are approved in arrival order; later leads take what the palette has left. Team-lead breaks ties.

## 3. Sizing

3–4 hires per track, **18 total, hard cap 18** (Warren's call). Thirteen archetypes at ≤2× each is 26 slots, so the cap fits with no repeats inside any team. Suggested cast — leads may adjust within the rules:

| Track | Hires | Suggested cast |
|---|---|---|
| 01 Product & Industry Research | 3 | Storyteller · Spreadsheet (lead's opposite) · Designated Skeptic |
| 02 AI & Security Architecture | 4 | Adversary · Principled Architect · Tinkerer (lead's opposite) · Designated Skeptic |
| 03 Engineering & Delivery | 4 | Detail Hawk (code reviewer) · Veteran · Enthusiast (lead's opposite) · Systems Cartographer (Go layout / service topology) |
| 04 Infra & DevOps | 3 | Newcomer · Enthusiast (lead's opposite) · Minimalist (scope guard) |
| 05 Data Ops | 4 | Systems Cartographer (multi-DB abstraction) · Storyteller (lead's opposite) · Detail Hawk (DDL nullability) · Spreadsheet (retention windows) |

Leads may wear a "hat" themselves in a three-hats run rather than hiring for it. Hires do **not** spawn subagents (nested-spawn depth cap); any research fan-out is done by the lead with haiku/low throwaway subagents.

### Deep research — leads only

"Deep research" means going past the project's own documents into primary sources: patents (Google Patents, USPTO/EPO), vendor and standards whitepapers, RFCs and FIDO/W3C specifications, academic papers, regulator guidance (NIST, GDPR texts), competitor technical docs. It is expensive, it is where hallucinated citations come from, and it shapes decisions other tracks build on — so it is **authorized and run only by track leads** (and team-lead). Rules:

1. A hire may *request* deep research from its lead with a one-line question and why the existing docs can't answer it; a hire never spawns a research subagent or performs deep research itself.
2. The lead decides whether the question earns it (does the answer change a decision another track depends on?), then fans out haiku/low or sonnet/low research subagents with a tightly scoped question each, in parallel.
3. Output lands as `<track-dir>/research-<topic>.md` with a source URL for every claim, marketing sources labeled as such, and an explicit "could not verify" list — the same standard the six first-wave research docs already follow.
4. Patents and whitepapers are cited for *what they claim*, never as proof the claim is true; the lead notes where a claim is contested.
5. The lead records the research in its `PLANNING.md` row and cites the file in the decision it informed.

299	<!-- WHAT IT LOOKS LIKE -->
300	<section id="demo">
301	  <div class="sec-head"><div><span class="eyebrow">What it looks like</span><h2>The live demo console</h2></div><p>Pulled as the forty-third story after the backlog closed, with one requirement added by the human: "make sure it looks good." A single page embedded in the API binary and served at <a href="https://loginid-takehome.uplifttech.org/demo">/demo</a> from the same origin, so it carries no secret and needs no CORS. You paste a QA credential that lives only in the tab; the page mints a real token and drives the real endpoints, showing each request as a curl line beside the raw response. These are the product owner's screenshots from her live review.</p></div>
302	  <div class="shots">
303	    <figure><img src="assets/demo-desktop.jpg" alt="Demo console at desktop width: token minted for three scopes, masked search beside full read with masked fields highlighted" loading="lazy"><figcaption>Three single-scope QA tokens minted, then the same profile read two ways: masked under <span class="mono">profile:search</span>, full under <span class="mono">profile:read:any</span>.</figcaption></figure>
304	    <figure><img src="assets/demo-desktop-2.jpg" alt="Demo console: the two 403 responses shown side by side with a byte-equality check, the 401, and the reason_code enum accepted and rejected" loading="lazy"><figcaption>Scope denial and policy denial return byte-identical 403 bodies (65 bytes, compared on the page); the 401 with no token; a recognised <span class="mono">reason_code</span> accepted, a made-up one rejected.</figcaption></figure>
305	    <figure class="phone"><img src="assets/demo-phone.jpg" alt="Demo console at phone width, stacked panels" loading="lazy"><figcaption>The same page at 390px: panels stack, nothing scrolls sideways.</figcaption></figure>
306	  </div>
307	  <p class="note" style="max-width:70ch">The review also recorded one honest limitation: the page sets every autofill hint the platform offers, and a browser-extension password manager still offered to save the QA credential, because extensions use their own heuristics. The control that holds is that the credentials are single-scope, low-value, and revocable by runbook.</p>
308	</section>
309	
310	<!-- ARCH -->
311	<section id="arch">
312	  <div class="sec-head"><div><span class="eyebrow">Software architecture</span><h2>What runs in the namespace</h2></div><p>Two Go binaries in three images. Watch the requests: a client mints a token at the issuer, calls the verifier, which checks the JWKS in-cluster and reads Postgres; the verifier alone may reach the connector; Vault feeds every pod its secrets at start. Hover a node for what it does.</p></div>
313	  <div class="panel arch-wrap"><canvas id="archCanvas" height="520"></canvas>
314	    <div class="arch-key"><span><i class="sw" style="background:var(--agent)"></i>client request</span><span><i class="sw" style="background:var(--sec)"></i>JWKS / Vault</span><span><i class="sw" style="background:var(--deploy)"></i>database</span><span><i class="sw" style="background:var(--inc)"></i>denied by NetworkPolicy</span></div></div>
315	  <div class="grid3" style="margin-top:22px">
316	    <div class="panel"><h3>Q1 · Data access</h3><p class="sub">DAO for user_profile and user_credential</p><p style="font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0">One Repository interface, three backends. A conformance suite runs identical assertions against real Postgres, CockroachDB, and SQLite containers in CI, including CockroachDB's serialization retry. Errors by SQLSTATE, never by message text.</p></div>
317	    <div class="panel"><h3>Q2 · REST + auth</h3><p class="sub">Search and retrieve, secured</p><p style="font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0">OAuth2 client credentials mint RS256 tokens with per-client audiences; the verifier checks issuer, audience, and JWKS with bounded staleness. Masked search; full reads need read:own or read:any with a closed reason code; scope and policy denials are byte-identical 403s.</p></div>
318	    <div class="panel"><h3>Q3 · IDP connector</h3><p class="sub">/auth and /identity to a vendor</p><p style="font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin:0">Vendor tokens fetched, used once, discarded. Uniform-timing failures, per-identity and overflow backoff; the failure-ratio limiter fired live in review. Reachable only from api-service.</p></div>
319	  </div>
320	</section>
321	
322	<!-- ORG -->
323	<section id="orgsec">
324	  <div class="sec-head"><div><span class="eyebrow">AI architecture</span><h2>The org that built it</h2></div><p>One PM session orchestrated five lead sessions, each a top-level Claude Code process with its own persona and CLAUDE.md. Leads hired three or four specialists with deliberately varied temperaments. Hires returned prose; leads wrote files; the PM alone merged and wrote Jira. An OpenClaw agent on another machine held the credentials.</p></div>
325	  <div class="grid3" id="patterns"></div>
326	</section>

334	<!-- LESSONS -->
335	<section id="lessons">
336	  <div class="sec-head"><div><span class="eyebrow">What the record shows</span><h2>Six patterns the day kept repeating</h2></div><p>Each caught by a review step, each closed with a check that fails in CI or at startup instead of in production.</p></div>
337	  <div class="lessons">
338	    <div><b>Config named, never set</b><p>Three times a binary read an environment variable no manifest set. Now every mode declares its required variables, refuses to start without them, and CI diffs manifests against that list.</p></div>
339	    <div><b>A pattern not carried forward</b><p>Five times a control on one component was missing on an identical new one: NetworkPolicy allow-lists, Forbid on a CronJob, a ServiceAccount per workload. Each got a test tying the new component to the rule.</p></div>
340	    <div><b>Verification that skips</b><p>CI was green for hours while every Postgres and CockroachDB test silently skipped. Fixtures now fail under CI and the containers are always present.</p></div>
341	    <div><b>The detector can fail too</b><p>"Diff against origin before declaring done" returned a false clean for untracked files. Five artifacts stranded; a <span class="mono">??</span> line is now unshipped work.</p></div>
342	    <div><b>Arithmetic outlives its assumption</b><p>An alert at window-plus-one-interval fired on every healthy cycle; a rolling-day skip rate was meaningless at a daily schedule. Both became functions of one interval constant.</p></div>
343	    <div><b>Fail loud, always</b><p>Vault Agent has two retry loops; one exits, the other retried forever and hung a pod in Init for seven minutes. The forced-failure test found it; the pod now crash-loops in thirty-six seconds.</p></div>
344	  </div>
345	</section>

  // ---- Panel 1: masked search vs. full read ----
  function findSampleId() {
    if (lastKnownProfileId) return Promise.resolve(lastKnownProfileId);
    if (!requireScope("profile:search")) return Promise.resolve(null);
    var body = JSON.stringify({ name: el("p1-query").value || "Review Demo" });
    return runFlow("p1-search", "POST", "/profiles/search", "profile:search", {
      contentType: "application/json",
      body: body,
      fieldTags: { phone: "masked", region: "masked" }
    }).then(function (result) {
      if (result.status !== 200) return null;
      try {
        var parsed = JSON.parse(result.text);

The honest answer is longer than a tool name, so this section gives the whole design: the layers, the model chosen for each seat and why, the rules that kept the agents from agreeing with each other, and a plain statement of which parts are Anthropic's documented patterns and which parts this project invented.</p></div>\n\n  <div class=\"stack\">\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The tool</div><div class=\"ls\">Claude Code</div></div><p>Anthropic's agentic command-line tool, chosen because the deliverable is itself an argument about AI-architecture judgement. Using the tool that is the subject of the question makes the workflow inspectable: every session was recorded, and those recordings are published below.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The briefs</div><div class=\"ls\">one root file, five track files</div></div><p>A root brief carries the assignment, the track boundaries and the working rules. Each of the five track directories carries its own brief, written to be self-sufficient: a fresh agent with no history can pick up that track from those two files alone. Context is retrieved when needed rather than all loaded at once, which is how a thirty-hour project stays inside a context window.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The sessions</div><div class=\"ls\">six top-level processes, not one</div></div><p>The project manager ran as one process. Each of the five track leads ran as its own separate process in its own terminal window, loading only its own brief and persona. They spoke to each other by message, but handed work over as files. A chat message signals that something is ready; it never carries the content.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The hires</div><div class=\"ls\">eighteen specialists, spawned by their lead</div></div><p>Each lead hired three or four specialists as sub-processes of its own session. Hires returned prose to their lead; leads wrote the files. Hires never spawned further agents, so the tree is exactly three deep and no work was ever orphaned under an agent nobody was watching.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The operator</div><div class=\"ls\">an agent on another machine, holding the credentials</div></div><p>Cluster and DNS credentials were never in this project's reach. A separate long-running agent on a different machine held them, took written requests, verified each one against the live cluster before acting, and refused anything the human had not authorised. It is visible in the recordings below asking the human directly before touching a production DNS zone.</p></div>\n  </div>\n\n  <div class=\"grid2\" style=\"margin-top:26px\">\n    <div class=\"panel\">\n      <h3>Which model sat in which seat, and why</h3>\n      <p class=\"sub\">One expensive seat where a wrong call propagates; cheaper, capable models everywhere the work is bounded.</p>\n      <div class=\"tblwrap\"><table><thead><tr><th>Seat</th><th>Model</th><th>Reasoning</th></tr></thead><tbody>\n        <tr><td>Orchestrator (PM)</td><td class=\"mono\">Fable&nbsp;5.1</td><td>Holds the only cross-track state. A boundary misjudged here costs five agents their day, not one.</td></tr>\n        <tr><td>Data-ops lead</td><td class=\"mono\">Opus&nbsp;5</td><td>Owns the schema and the database interface every other track builds against. The most expensive decision to reverse late.</td></tr>\n        <tr><td>Four other leads</td><td class=\"mono\">Sonnet&nbsp;5</td><td>Design and delivery against a brief that is already concrete. Capable is enough; the ceiling is not the bottleneck.</td></tr>\n        <tr><td>Eighteen hires</td><td class=\"mono\">Sonnet&nbsp;5</td><td>Bounded, single-purpose seats: one attack tree, one cold read, one objection.</td></tr>\n        <tr><td>Research fan-out</td><td class=\"mono\">Haiku&nbsp;4.5</td><td>Narrow, verifiable, throwaway lookups run in parallel. Exploration noise never re-enters the coordinator's context, only the condensed answer does.</td></tr>\n      </tbody></table></div>\n      <p class=\"note\">The security lead wrote that reasoning up before any of it ran, then recorded where the harness refused to cooperate: there is no per-agent effort setting, so the planned medium tier collapsed upward into high. The plan says so rather than pretending the design survived contact intact.</p>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"panel\">\n      <h3>Why the agents were given different personalities</h3>\n      <p class=\"sub\">Not decoration. A countermeasure against a specific failure.</p>\n      <p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2)\">Five leads and a manager, all started from the same base model, land in the same temperament by default: serious, careful, thorough. Agreement between agents who think alike is not evidence that a decision is good, it is evidence that nobody in the room was built to disagree.</p>\n      <p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin-top:10px\">So variance was engineered. Thirteen archetypes, each with a stated blind spot. No archetype more than twice across the whole organisation and never twice inside one team. Every team had to contain its own lead's temperament opposite. At least three hires had to be playful and bold, because not one lead was. Each hire's brief states plainly what it pushes back on and where its own judgement fails.</p>\n      <p style=\"font-size:14px;color:var(--ink2);margin-top:10px\">The effect is visible in the record: the adversary keeps finding attacks, the minimalist keeps deleting scope, and the newcomer keeps asking the question everyone else had stopped asking.</p>\n    </div>\n  </div>\n\n  <div class=\"sec-head\" style=\"margin-top:56px\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Six protocols</span><h3 style=\"font-size:clamp(22px,2.4vw,30px)\">The patterns the teams actually ran</h3></div><p>Each is a small protocol with named seats and a required artefact. Every run ends in a dated decision file that records the positions, the rebuttals, the ruling, and which model each seat ran on. Chat is never the record.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"grid3\" id=\"patterns\"></div>\n\n  <div class=\"sec-head\" style=\"margin-top:56px\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">Honesty about the claim</span><h3 style=\"font-size:clamp(22px,2.4vw,30px)\">Did any of this actually help?</h3></div><p>The security track put that question to its own debate protocol rather than asserting an answer, and the ruling went against the broad claim. What follows is what the record supports, what it does not, and what it cost.</p></div>\n  <div class=\"claim\">\n    <div class=\"a\"><div class=\"hd\">What the evidence supports</div>\n      <ul>\n        <li>A seat whose brief is structurally different produces findings the previous seat could not. The alternatives seat deleted two attack paths outright by removing the token cache, where every prior seat had proposed hardening it.</li>\n        <li>A reader with no stake in the text catches what its author cannot. Cold reads of finished hand-offs returned real gaps every time they ran.</li>\n        <li>Independent review caught a rate-limit control that was a silent no-op for the broadest scope. Neither the author of the fix nor the objector it answered had seen it, because both were reasoning inside the question as posed.</li>\n      </ul></div>\n    <div class=\"b\"><div class=\"hd\">What it does not support</div>\n      <p>That multi-agent debate beats one strong session in general. Nobody ran that comparison. The defensible claim is a mechanism, not a statistic: self-review interrogates the answer it already committed to, rather than the frame that produced the question. The losing side of that debate is preserved in the repository alongside the winning one.</p>\n      <p style=\"margin-top:10px\">Null results are counted in the same tallies as the hits, at the skeptic's insistence.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"c\"><div class=\"hd\">The failure worth stealing</div>\n      <p>Independence decayed silently. A reviewer assigned at planning time had, by the time the review ran, authored the very content she was set to check, through two intervening debates. Nothing flagged it; the roster still said she was independent.</p>\n      <p style=\"margin-top:10px\">The lesson generalises past AI: independence is a property to verify at the moment a review runs, not a role assigned once and trusted. It was caught before the review ran, and the reviewers were reassigned.</p></div>\n  </div>\n\n  <div class=\"panel\" style=\"margin-top:26px\">\n    <h3>The rules that made it auditable</h3>\n    <div class=\"grid3\" style=\"margin-top:6px\">\n      <div><b style=\"font-family:var(--display)\">Everything names its author</b><p style=\"font-size:13.5px;color:var(--ink2);margin:4px 0 0\">Every commit, pull request and ticket transition names the persona who did the work and the seat they sat in. Not \"the team\", not \"Claude\". You can trace any line of code to the agent that wrote it and the reviewer that passed it.</p></div>\n      <div><b style=\"font-family:var(--display)\">Hand-offs are files</b><p style=\"font-size:13.5px;color:var(--ink2);margin:4px 0 0\">A hand-off must let the receiver act without re-deriving the sender's reasoning. It gets tested by giving it to an agent that was not in the conversation and seeing what they cannot do.</p></div>\n      <div><b style=\"font-family:var(--display)\">Done means deployed and reviewed</b><p style=\"font-size:13.5px;color:var(--ink2);margin:4px 0 0\">No story closed on a passing test. It closed when the product owner exercised it at the public URL. Nineteen stories failed that review before they passed it.</p></div>\n    </div>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<!-- WATCH -->\n<section id=\"agents\">\n  <div class=\"sec-head\"><div><span class=\"eyebrow\">The receipts</span><h2>Watch the agents work, then read every word</h2></div><p>These are unedited screen recordings of the terminal while the organisation ran, screenshots of the operator agent working on the live cluster, and a complete archive of every session: each message, each tool call, each result, with credentials redacted. Nothing here is a reconstruction.</p></div>\n\n  <div class=\"vids\">\n    <div class=\"vid\"><video src=\"media/demo-video-1.mp4\" poster=\"assets/poster-video-1.jpg\" controls preload=\"none\" playsinline muted></video><div class=\"cap\"><b>A security lead briefing four hires at once</b><p>The adversary, the architect, the tinkerer and the skeptic each report back in their own voice and then hold, waiting to be called into a seat. The lead rules. Thirty-six seconds.</p></div></div>\n    <div class=\"vid\"><video src=\"media/demo-video-3.mp4\" poster=\"assets/poster-video-3.jpg\" controls preload=\"none\" playsinline muted></video><div class=\"cap\"><b>The manager running five tracks at once</b><p>Messages arriving from the engineering and infrastructure leads, a consistency pass reporting five stale restatements, and the operator agent being contacted on another machine. The honest answer is longer than a tool name, so this section gives the whole design: the layers, the model chosen for each seat and why, the rules that kept the agents from simply agreeing with each other, and a plain statement of which parts are Anthropic's documented patterns and which parts this project invented.</p></div>\n\n  <div class=\"stack\">\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The tool</div><div class=\"ls\">Claude Code</div></div><p>Anthropic's agentic command-line tool, chosen because the deliverable is itself an argument about AI-architecture judgement. Each of the five track directories carries its own brief, written to be self-sufficient: a fresh agent with no history can pick up that track from those two files alone. Context is retrieved when it is needed rather than all loaded at once, which is how a thirty-hour project stays inside a context window.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The sessions</div><div class=\"ls\">six top-level processes, not one</div></div><p>The project manager ran as one process. Each of the five track leads ran as its own separate process in its own terminal window, loading only its own brief and its own persona. They spoke to each other by message, but handed work over as files. A message signals that something is ready; it never carries the content.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The hires</div><div class=\"ls\">eighteen specialists, spawned by their lead</div></div><p>Each lead hired three or four specialists as sub-processes of its own session. Hires never spawned further agents, so the tree is exactly three deep and no work was ever orphaned under an agent nobody was watching.</p></div>\n    <div class=\"layer\"><div><div class=\"lt\">The operator</div><div class=\"ls\">an agent on another machine, holding the credentials</div></div><p>Cluster and DNS credentials were never in this project's reach.
